PRELIMINARY STATEMENT 
The Public Utility Law Project of New York (“PULP”) hereby submits this Statement in 
Opposition of the Joint Proposal (“JP”) filed with the New York State Public Service Commission 
(“PSC” or the “Commission”) by The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY and 
KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“the Companies”) and signatory parties in 
these proceedings on April 9, 2024.1  As a nonprofit organization tasked with advocating on behalf 
of low-income utility customers, PULP strongly opposes the JP as filed in this case.  
The impact of the rate increases proposed in the JP will harm customers, especially low-
income customers, who are already struggling to afford their bills. While PULP appreciates the 
steps that parties have taken in considering consumer protections in this agreement, those included 
in the JP do not provide sufficient protection to vulnerable consumers and do not offset the 
incredible financial impacts those same customers will face. PULP will expand upon our concerns 
below.  
PROCEDURAL HISTORY 
The Companies filed their request for new rates on April 28, 2023. PULP and other parties 
engaged in discovery surrounding the filing. On August 31, 2023, PULP joined other parties in 
filing direct testimony the purpose of which was to: (1) provide evidence that the delivery rate 
increases requested by the Companies would jeopardize low-income customers; (2) make 
recommendations to reduce the scale of the Companies requests; and (3) propose improvements 
 
1 The Signatory Parties in these proceedings are: The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY, 
KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid, Department of Public Service Staff, Environmental Defense 
Fund, NRG Energy, Inc., and the City of New York.  


--- Page 3 ---
 
 
2 
to the customer service experience of vulnerable customers. On September 19, 2023, PULP filed 
corrections to our testimony. 
On September 22, 2023, PULP and other parties filed Rebuttal Testimony. On September 
26, 2023, the Companies filed with the parties and the Secretary to the Commission a Notice of 
Impending Settlement Negotiations. Settlement negotiations began on October 11, 2023, and 
continued through March 2024. A joint proposal was filed by the Companies on April 9, 2024.  
PULP now submits this Statement in Opposition, which sets forth our basis and rationale 
for urging the Administrative Law Judges (“ALJs”) to recommend to the Commission that the JP 
be rejected or modified to reflect the recommendations discussed below. PULP notes that silence 
on any matter contained in the JP does not indicate PULP’s support. PULP reserves the right to 
discuss additional matters in future statements, briefs, or during a future Evidentiary Hearing. 
STANDARD OF REVIEW 
According to the Commission’s Settlement Guidelines, “[a]ll Commission decisions, 
including those pertaining to proposed settlements, must be, and appear to be, just, reasonable, and 
in the public interest.”2 The elements considered in determining the “public interest” include: 
1. consistency with the law and regulatory economic, social and environmental State 
and Commission policies; 
2. whether the terms of the joint proposal compare favorably with the likely result of a 
fully litigated case and produce a result within the range of reasonable outcomes;  
 
2 Cases 90-M-0225 and 92-M-0138, Opinion, Order and Resolution Adopting Settlement Procedures and  
Guidelines (Mar. 24, 1992), at 30. 


--- Page 4 ---
 
 
3 
3. whether the joint proposal fairly balances the interests of ratepayers, investors and the 
long-term soundness of the utility; and  
4. whether the joint proposal provides a rational basis for the Commission’s decision. 
Additionally, Commissioners are guided in their assessment by two additional factors. 
First, the completeness of the record, and second, whether the settlement is contested.3 
ARGUMENT 
THE BILL IMPACTS ASSOCIATED WITH THE JOINT PROPOSAL ARE NOT JUST, 
REASONABLE, OR WITHIN THE PUBLIC INTEREST 
 
If approved by the Commission, the JP will result in rate impacts that are untenable for 
residential customers. This is especially true of low-income residential customers who are not 
currently enrolled in the Energy Affordability Program (“EAP”). A number of factors including 
the negotiated Return on Equity (“ROE”), revenue requirements, and insufficient earnings sharing 
contribute to rate impacts that will harm residential customers.  
I. 
The Bill Impacts Associated with the Joint Proposal Fall Out of Line with 
Regulatory Economic and Social Policies of the State and the Commission 
The Public Service Commission has long recognized the important policy objective of 
“protecting access to energy services for those who may face financial difficulties.”4 KEDNY and 
KEDLI customers are already struggling immensely to keep up with their bills. The bill impacts 
proposed under the JP would exacerbate the issue of affordability and decrease access to energy 
services for low-income New Yorkers. As of March 2024, 194,915 residential KEDNY customers 
 
3 Id.  
4 See, The Department of Public Service, Energy Affordability Program, https://dps.ny.gov/energy-affordability-
program.  


--- Page 5 ---
 
 
4 
and 50,150 residential KEDLI customers were sixty or more days behind on their bills.5 The 
amount owed to KEDNY by customers in March 2024 was $153,780,726, and at KEDLI, the total 
was $40,215,904. That same month, 251 residential KEDNY customer accounts were terminated 
for non-payment and 151 were terminated for nonpayment at KEDLI.6 
Based on data included in the American Community Survey for 2021, there were 340,000 
KEDNY and 89,000 KEDLI households whose income falls below the Home Energy Assistance 
Program (“HEAP”) eligibility limit, categorizing them as low-income.7 However, for various 
reasons, only an estimated 16 percent of these customers are enrolled in KEDLI’s EAP program 
and only 44 percent of these customers are enrolled in KEDNY’s.8 While low-income customers 
enrolled in EAP will be shielded from some of the burdens associated with the potential rate 
increases, low-income customers who are not enrolled, as well as moderate income customers, 
will experience incredible unaffordability.  
In the first year of the proposed rate plan alone, average usage residential customers could 
see between 8.5 percent to 134.41 percent delivery bill increases depending on whether they are 
EAP enrolled and whether they are KEDLI or KEDNY customers.9 Average usage non- EAP

information provided in the IRs reflect rates that would be effective June 1 with a make-whole provision to April 1, 
and is not reflective of any additional compression of rates if an Order is not issued prior to June 1.)

customers will face delivery bill impacts between 24.49 percent and 27.81 percent.10 Because 
many low-income customers are not enrolled in EAP, this subset of customers will face these bill 
impacts without the assistance an EAP discount might provide, which could leave them at greater 
risk of falling into arrears or facing loss of service.  
II. 
The ROE Included in the Joint Proposal is not in the Public Interest Because in 
Practice, a Fixed ROE has the Potential to Harm Ratepayers 
The harmful bill impacts discussed above are driven in part by an ROE of 9.35 percent that 
is set for the entire three-year term of the rate plan. Each basis point of the ROE in this case (i.e., 
the difference between 9.34 percent and 9.35 percent) is equivalent to revenues of $490,000 for 
KEDNY and $316,000 for KEDLI in Rate Year One.11 These amounts increase to $580,000 and 
$375,000, respectively, in Rate Year Three.12 Cumulatively, the ROE proffered in this JP will 
permit the Companies to collect millions of dollars from households that are already facing 
significant financial hardship. Absent a mechanism for recalculating the ROE for this case on an 
annual basis, the Commission should modify the JP to include an ROE set at the lower of (i) 9.35 
percent or (ii) the result of Staff's ROE Model13 run with the most recent available data. 
Signatory parties will likely argue that the ROE represents a negotiated agreement that will 
enable the companies to attract the capital necessary to provide safe and reliable service at just and 
 
10 See, Case 23-G-0225 et al., Appendices 1-4 KEDNY KEDLI Joint Proposal, Appendix 3 Schedule 4 and 
Appendix 4 Schedule 4 (Apr. 9, 2024). (Indicating that average usage non- EAP residential heating customers at 
KEDNY and KEDLI would experience increases of 24.49 percent and 26.44 percent, respectively. Average usage 
non-EAP residential non-heating customers at KEDNY and KEDLI would experience increases of 26.36 percent and 
27.81 percent, respectively).  
11 Cases 23-G-0225 et. al., Joint Proposal (Apr. 9, 2024) footnote 4 at 8.  
12 Id.  
13 “Staff ROE Model” refers to the methodology by which DPS Staff arrives at a recommended ROE in an 
individual rate case. See, e.g., Cases 23-G-0225 et. al., Testimony of Kwaku Duah, (Sept. 1, 2023) at 100: “I 
determined my recommended 9.1 percent cost of equity by weighting: (1) the 8.52 percent average DCF result from 
my proxy group two-thirds; and (2) the 10.18 percent average of the two CAPM results one-third.” 


--- Page 7 ---
 
 
6 
reasonable rates. Additionally, signatory parties have often argued that locking in an ROE for a 
multi-year rate plan is just as big a risk to the company as it would be to customers.14  
Recent history has not borne out this risk. In his initial and rebuttal testimonies in these 
proceedings, PULP witness Yates presented his analysis of KEDNY and KEDLI ratepayer costs 
attributable to fixed, authorized ROEs across the span of three rate cases.15 The results of his 
analysis suggest that the Companies’ ratepayers incurred up to $481 million more in the cost of 
their gas service because of an authorized ROE that was locked in for several years rather than 
updated to account for changing economic factors.16 
A Joint Proposal that perpetuates a mechanism that consistently overcharges ratepayers to 
the benefit of a utility’s investors cannot be said to “fairly balance” the interests of ratepayers, 
investors, and the long-term soundness of the utility. To partially rectify this historic imbalance, 
the Commission should require that DPS Staff re-run its ROE Model with the most recent available 
data, adopting the output of the model as the new authorized ROE for the Companies if it falls 
below 9.35 percent.  
 
 
14 See, e.g., Cases 22-E-0064 et. al., Staff Statement in Reply, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the 
Rates, Charges, Rules and Regulations of Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. for Electric Service 
(Apr. 5, 2023) at 20: “Given that multi-year rate plans generally include a stay-out premium to reflect the inherent 
risk assumed by the utility in foregoing general rate relief for at least two years, a 9.25% ROE is reasonable.”; Cases 
22-E-0317 et. al., Staff Statement in Support of the Joint Proposal, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to 
the Rates, Charges, Rules and Regulations of New York State Electric & Gas Corporation for Electric Service (Jun. 
27, 2023) at 21: “As opposed to a single rate year, the extended term of the Joint Proposal inherently carries more 
financial risk as investors are subject to additional risk that economic conditions will change and the actual cost of 
capital could increase during the three-year term.” 
15 Supra note 7, at 37.

III. 
The Joint Proposal Unfairly Prioritizes the Interests of Investors Over Those of 
Ratepayers by Limiting Earning Sharing 
The terms of the Earning Sharing Mechanism ("ESM") included in the Joint Proposal are 
unreasonably weighted in favor of the Companies' shareholders over customers and should be 
modified by the Commission.  
Under the Joint Proposal, if the Companies' ROE exceeds 9.85 percent in a given rate 
year—an amount already 50 basis points above the allowed ROE of 9.35 percent—shareholders 
and customers will evenly "share" the excess earnings. If the ROE exceeds 10.35 percent in a given 
rate year, customers will receive 75 percent of the excess earnings. Finally, if the ROE exceeds 
10.85 percent in a given rate year, customers will receive 90 percent of the excess earnings.17 
It is important to highlight that at all tiers of the ESM—even the initial tier in which the 
excess earnings are shared “evenly” between ratepayers and the Companies—the amounts 
awarded to customers are set to be deferred to pay down future costs. The same is not true for the 
Companies and their shareholders. It is only in the second and third tiers of the ESM, when the 
ROE exceeds 100 basis points above the allowed ROE, that the Companies will need to set aside 
half of their excess earnings “to reduce regulatory asset balances associated with Site Investigation 
and Remediation (“SIR”) activities.”18 This language matches in substance that of the Companies’ 
2019 Joint Proposal.19 However, costs associated with SIR activities—costs that will be borne 
almost exclusively by customers—have grown considerably since the Companies’ most recent rate 
case. 
 
17 Supra note 11, at 22. 
18 Id. 
19 Cases 19-G-0309 et. al., Joint Proposal (May 14, 2021) at 39. 


--- Page 9 ---
 
 
8 
In his initial testimony in these proceedings, PULP witness Yates discussed this concerning 
increase in the balance of KEDNY’s Deferred Environmental Costs account.20 As of 2022, 
KEDNY estimated that it will need to spend $1.675 billion on future environmental clean-up 
costs.21 This massive financial burden—an amount unequaled by any other utility in the state—is 
set to fall solely on the shoulders of KEDNY’s ratepayers, absent 50 percent of KEDNY’s share 
of excess earnings under the ESM. These extraordinary and unprecedented costs warrant a 
deviation from the terms of the current rate plan. 
In its 2012 Order Concerning Costs for Site Investigation and Remediation (“SIR Order”), 
the Commission established a requirement (1) that utilities file annual SIR reports, (2) that the 
utilities formalize and file with the Commission an inventory of SIR cost containment “best 
practices,” and (3) that in future rate filings a utility provide sworn testimony (a) that its 
remediation process is in compliance with applicable timetables and requirements, (b) discussing 
its SIR cost control efforts, and (c) indicating the results of any internal process that it may have 
conducted with respect to reviewing its SIR procedures.22 The proceeding that ultimately resulted 
in the Commission’s SIR Order was originally established “to examine, on a generic basis, the 
treatment of costs for utilities’ [SIR] of hazardous waste, primarily at manufactured gas plant 
(MGP) sites.”23 In establishing the inquiry, the Commission “anticipated the growth of these costs 
and sough to mitigate the concomitant burden on ratepayers[.]”24 While the Commission ultimately 
declined to establish a generic policy regarding SIR expense allocation, it explicitly retained “the 
 
20 Supra note 7, at 56. 
21 Id. 
22 Case 11-M-0034, Order Concerning Costs for Site Investigation and Remediation, Proceeding on Motion of the 
Commission to Commence a Review and Evaluation of the Treatment of the State’s Regulated Utilities’ Site 
Investigation and Remediation (SIR) Costs (Nov. 28, 2012) at 32. 
23 Id. at 1. 
24 Id. 


--- Page 10 ---
 
 
9 
flexibility to tailor the rate treatment of SIR costs to the concrete conditions of each utility.“25 
Moreover, the Commission recognized that “sharing of some proportion may serve as an incentive 
to constrain SIR costs for utilities that appear to need such incentive.”26   
In light of the historically high cost of KEDNY’s future environmental remediation, the 
Commission should exercise the flexibility it retained in its 2012 SIR Order by altering the terms 
of the Joint Proposal to require that both KEDNY’s share, and the customer share of earnings in 
excess of the authorized ROE be used to pay down the Deferred Environmental Costs included in 
Account 182.3.  
CONCLUSION 
For the reasons discussed above, PULP submits our Statement of Opposition. PULP 
urges the ALJs to recommend to the PSC that it adopt the above modifications to the JP in any 
order resulting from these proceedings.

The bill tables presented above depict a snapshot of average usage for an Energy Affordability 
Program (“EAP”) customer.  It is important to note, however, that these tables are not necessarily 
reflective of the average bill increase a typical EAP customer may experience for the following 
reasons: 

The average bill assumes, for KEDLI, as an example, 79 therms per month.  Over the 
course of the Rate Year, more than half of the months have usage lower than 79 therms, 
however.   

The calculation of annual EAP discounts is based on a hybrid average low-income bill 
that combines an historical three-year weighted monthly average commodity bill with a 
forecast year delivery bill and assumes three-year historic average usage.  As a result, 
there will be differences when comparing EAP discounts that are intended to keep the 
average bill at the 6% energy burden and what customers may actually experience 
throughout that Rate Year depending on actual usage each month. 
Statement Of Opposition Of Public Utility Law Project Of New York, Inc. To Joint Proposal 
                                                           Cases 23-G-0225 et al
                                                                    Appendix  1
                                                                    Page 3 of 4


--- Page 14 ---
Form 103

Specific to KEDNY, the calculation of Heating EAP discounts for Rate Years One 
through Three was limited to the 2% budget cap in accordance with the EAP Order.  As a 
result, the 6% energy burden was not able to be maintained which is why there are higher 
monthly bill impacts for KEDNY Heating EAP customers.   

For KEDLI, the 2% budget cap was not a factor.  
The information above reflects rates that would be effective June 1 with a make-whole provision to 
April 1, and is not reflective of any additional compression of rates if an Order is not issued prior to 
June 1.

bill that combines an historical three-year weighted monthly average commodity bill 
with a forecast year delivery bill and assumes three-year historic average usage.  As 
a result, there will be differences when comparing EAP discounts that are intended 
to keep the average bill at the 6% energy burden and what customers may actually 
experience throughout that Rate Year depending on actual usage each month. 

Specific to KEDNY, the calculation of Heating EAP discounts for Rate Years One 
through Three was limited to the 2% budget cap in accordance with the EAP Order.  
As a result, the 6% energy burden was not able to be maintained which is why we 
are seeing higher monthly bill impacts for KEDNY Heating EAP customers.   

For KEDLI, the 2% budget cap was not a factor.  
Name of Respondent:
Date of Reply:
Kellie Smith on behalf of National Grid
